People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

Well… I guess I’m here because when I talk, it kind of gets mixed up. I don’t mean to be difficult. I’m trying to answer right, you know?

Not all the time, I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but… it’s like the pieces don’t stay in order.\n\nI’ll be thinking about one thing, like the keys, and then it jumps to the jail, then the prayer rug, then chess pieces, and by the time I’m talking, people tell me they can’t follow me. I’m trying to make sense, though. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you been using any substances, like alcohol or drugs, around the time your speech started getting mixed up?\n\nPatient: Um… no, I don’t think so. I mean, not that I’ve noticed. I’m not trying to hide anything from you, Doctor, I just… I don’t really have anything like that to point to.\n\nIf you mean like drinking or drugs making me talk funny, I don’t think that’s what was going on. Is that okay to say?\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant changes or problems after stopping or reducing the use of any substances?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so, Doctor. I haven’t noticed anything like that after stopping or cutting back on anything.\n\nI mean, I’m not trying to give the wrong answer, but I don’t really have a substance thing I can connect it to. If there’s something specific you mean, you can ask me, but nothing comes to mind.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your thoughts are racing or moving very quickly when they get jumbled?\n\nPatient: Yeah… sometimes, yes. I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but it can feel really fast.\n\nLike there’s a whole crowd talking in my head at once, and it jumps around before I can catch one thought. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often find it hard to stick to one topic when you're thinking about something?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I do, sometimes. I try to stay on one thing, but it kind of slips away from me.\n\nLike I’ll start explaining about the keys, and then somehow I’m talking about jail, or the prayer rug, or chess pieces… and then people look at me like they can’t follow what I’m saying. I don’t mean to do it. I’m trying to answer right, it just gets mixed up when I talk. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of time when you didn't use any substances at all?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that, Doctor. I don’t think I’ve had a “substance” period like that, if that’s what you mean.\n\nI mean… I’m not using anything like that, so I guess yes? But I don’t want to say it wrong. I haven’t noticed a time where stopping or not using something made a big difference.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your thoughts are disconnected from reality?\n\nPatient: I mean… I don’t want to say I’m “disconnected,” exactly. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n18\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF23, F25.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I guess I’m here because when I talk, it kind of gets mixed up. I’m trying to explain one thing, like the keys, and then I’m talking about jail, and then a prayer rug, or chess pieces… and people tell me they can’t really follow what I’m saying. I don’t mean to be difficult. I’m trying to answer right, you know?\n\nDoctor: When did you first notice that your speech was getting mixed up like this?\n\nPatient: I think… around two years or so. Maybe a little more—like two years and five months, if I’m counting right. It was after that brief detention thing, the misunderstanding at the building. I don’t want to make it sound bigger than it was, but that’s when people started saying my words weren’t coming out right. Does that answer it okay?\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing any hearing or seeing things that aren't there?\n\nPatient: Um… not seeing, not really. Hearing, maybe. I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, because I know that sounds bad, but sometimes I hear a man’s voice, like it’s coming from the vents or the mop closet. It’ll say my name, or say I ruined everything.\n\nAnd it sounds… real. Like somebody is standing right next to me. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to keep your thoughts organized in your mind before you try to express them in words?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I try to keep it straight in my head, but then when I start talking it goes sideways. Like I’ll be trying to explain about the keys, and then somehow I’m on the jail thing, then a prayer rug, then chess pieces… and people look at me like they can’t follow it.\n\nI don’t mean to do that. I’m trying to answer the right thing. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your thoughts are jumbled or disconnected from each other even when you're not trying to express them in words?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it does get kind of jumbled even before I say it. Not all the time, I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but… it’s like the pieces don’t stay in order.\n\nI’ll be thinking about one thing, like the keys, and then it jumps to the jail, then the prayer rug, then chess pieces, and by the time I’m talking, people tell me they can’t follow me. I’m trying to make sense, though. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you been using any substances, like alcohol or drugs, around the time your speech started getting mixed up?\n\nPatient: Um… no, I don’t think so. I mean, not that I’ve noticed. I’m not trying to hide anything from you, Doctor, I just… I don’t really have anything like that to point to.\n\nIf you mean like drinking or drugs making me talk funny, I don’t think that’s what was going on. Is that okay to say?\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant changes or problems after stopping or reducing the use of any substances?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so, Doctor. I haven’t noticed anything like that after stopping or cutting back on anything.\n\nI mean, I’m not trying to give the wrong answer, but I don’t really have a substance thing I can connect it to. If there’s something specific you mean, you can ask me, but nothing comes to mind.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your thoughts are racing or moving very quickly when they get jumbled?\n\nPatient: Yeah… sometimes, yes. I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but it can feel really fast.\n\nLike there’s a whole crowd talking in my head at once, and it jumps around before I can catch one thought. I’ll go from bills, to jail, to the mosque, to cleaning chemicals… and then I’m already on the next thing. It’s hard to slow it down when it gets like that. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often find it hard to stick to one topic when you're thinking about something?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I do, sometimes. I try to stay on one thing, but it kind of slips away from me.\n\nLike I’ll start explaining about the keys, and then somehow I’m talking about jail, or the prayer rug, or chess pieces… and then people look at me like they can’t follow what I’m saying. I don’t mean to do it. I’m trying to answer right, it just gets mixed up when I talk. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of time when you didn't use any substances at all?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that, Doctor. I don’t think I’ve had a “substance” period like that, if that’s what you mean.\n\nI mean… I’m not using anything like that, so I guess yes? But I don’t want to say it wrong. I haven’t noticed a time where stopping or not using something made a big difference.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your thoughts are disconnected from reality?\n\nPatient: I mean… I don’t want to say I’m “disconnected,” exactly. That sounds bad. But there are times where I’m sure something is happening, even if other people tell me it’s not.\n\nLike one time my supervisor showed me there wasn’t anything on the camera, and I could see what he was saying… but I still felt like the building people had marked me somehow. Like the hallway lights, and little looks they gave each other, were warnings about me.\n\nI know that probably doesn’t sound right. I’m just trying to explain it honestly. Is that the kind of thing you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a problem with alcohol or drug use in the past?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so, Doctor. I mean, I’ve had drinks here and there like people do, but I wouldn’t say it was a problem.\n\nAnd drugs, no, not really. I don’t want to make it sound like I’m perfect or anything, but I can’t think of a time where alcohol or drugs were the reason things were going wrong. Is that answering it the right way?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like you needed to cut down on your drinking or drug use?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I mean… I’ve never felt like I had to cut down, not in that way.\n\nI don’t really use drugs, and drinking isn’t something I feel pulled to do. So I don’t think that’s been an issue for me. Is that okay to say?\n\nDoctor: Do you have difficulty completing tasks or projects due to your thoughts being disorganized?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that does happen. I don’t mean to be difficult or anything, but when I try to explain something, it can get all tangled up.\n\nLike I’ll start talking about the keys, and then somehow I’m on jail, and then the prayer rug, and then chess pieces… and in my head it feels connected, but people tell me they can’t follow what I’m saying. So I guess, yes, it makes it hard to finish what I’m trying to do or explain.\n\nI’m trying to answer it the right way.\n\nDoctor: Do you have times when your thoughts are organized and you can express yourself clearly?\n\nPatient: Sometimes, yeah… I think there are times I can say things clear enough. I don’t want to make it sound like I’m always mixed up.\n\nBut what brings me here is that my talking does get all tangled. I try to explain about the keys, and then it goes to the jail, then the prayer rug, then chess pieces… and people tell me they can’t follow me. In my head I’m trying to make sense, but it doesn’t always come out that way.\n\nIs that what you mean, Doctor?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your mixed-up thoughts and speech are causing you a lot of distress or problems in your daily life or relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it has caused problems, more than I like to admit.\n\nI try to keep it together, but I’ve missed shifts and lost some cleaning work because people said they couldn’t rely on me, or they couldn’t understand what I was trying to say. And I stopped going to mosque sometimes, and family gatherings too, because I feel embarrassed… like people are looking at me or getting frustrated.\n\nThere have been times it got bad enough that other people had to call for emergency help. I don’t like saying that, Doctor, but… yes, it has affected my life a lot. Is that the kind of thing you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any extremely stressful events or significant changes in your life around the time your speech started getting mixed up?\n\nPatient: I think… yeah, there was something around then. I don’t want to make it sound like an excuse, Doctor, but after that brief detention, the misunderstanding at the building… that’s when I started noticing my speech getting more mixed up.\n\nIt’s been going on about… two years and five months now, I think. Around there. I hope that answers what you’re asking.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any other symptoms like the voice you mentioned, such as seeing things that aren't there, besides the voice you hear?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I really see things, Doctor… not like that. At least, I don’t want to say something wrong.\n\nIt’s more the voice. Sometimes I hear a man’s voice coming from the vents, or from the mop closet, saying my name… or saying I ruined everything. And it sounds real, like someone is standing right there beside me. I don’t want to make it sound bigger than it was, but that’s when people started saying my words weren’t coming out right. It’ll say my name, or say I ruined everything.\n\nAnd it sounds… real. Like somebody is standing right next to me. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to keep your thoughts organized in your mind before you try to express them in words?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I try to keep it straight in my head, but then when I start talking it goes sideways. I’m trying to answer the right thing. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your thoughts are jumbled or disconnected from each other even when you're not trying to express them in words?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it does get kind of jumbled even before I say it. I’m trying to make sense, though. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you been using any substances, like alcohol or drugs, around the time your speech started getting mixed up?\n\nPatient: Um… no, I don’t think so. I mean, not that I’ve noticed. I’m not trying to hide anything from you, Doctor, I just… I don’t really have anything like that to point to.\n\nIf you mean like drinking or drugs making me talk funny, I don’t think that’s what was going on. I mean, I try to keep it straight in my head, but then when I start talking it goes sideways. Like I’ll be trying to explain about the keys, and then somehow I’m on the jail thing, then a prayer rug, then chess pieces… and people look at me like they can’t follow it.\n\nI don’t mean to do that. I’m trying to answer the right thing. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your thoughts are jumbled or disconnected from each other even when you're not trying to express them in words?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it does get kind of jumbled even before I say it. Not all the time, I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but… it’s like the pieces don’t stay in order.\n\nI’ll be thinking about one thing, like the keys, and then it jumps to the jail, then the prayer rug, then chess pieces, and by the time I’m talking, people tell me they can’t follow me. I’m trying to make sense, though. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you been using any substances, like alcohol or drugs, around the time your speech started getting mixed up?\n\nPatient: Um… no, I don’t think so. I mean, not that I’ve noticed. I’m not trying to hide anything from you, Doctor, I just… I don’t really have anything like that to point to.\n\nIf you mean like drinking or drugs making me talk funny, I don’t think that’s what was going on. Is that okay to say?\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant changes or problems after stopping or reducing the use of any substances?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so, Doctor. I haven’t noticed anything like that after stopping or cutting back on anything.\n\nI mean, I’m not trying to give the wrong answer, but I don’t really have a substance thing I can connect it to. If there’s something specific you mean, you can ask me, but nothing comes to mind.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your thoughts are racing or moving very quickly when they get jumbled?\n\nPatient: Yeah… sometimes, yes. I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but it can feel really fast.\n\nLike there’s a whole crowd talking in my head at once, and it jumps around before I can catch one thought. I’ll go from bills, to jail, to the mosque, to cleaning chemicals… and then I’m already on the next thing. It’s hard to slow it down when it gets like that. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often find it hard to stick to one topic when you're thinking about something?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I do, sometimes. I try to stay on one thing, but it kind of slips away from me.\n\nLike I’ll start explaining about the keys, and then somehow I’m talking about jail, or the prayer rug, or chess pieces… and then people look at me like they can’t follow what I’m saying. I don’t mean to do it. I’m trying to answer right, it just gets mixed up when I talk. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of time when you didn't use any substances at all?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that, Doctor. I don’t think I’ve had a “substance” period like that, if that’s what you mean.\n\nI mean… I’m not using anything like that, so I guess yes? But I don’t want to say it wrong. I haven’t noticed a time where stopping or not using something made a big difference.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your thoughts are disconnected from reality?\n\nPatient: I mean… I don’t want to say I’m “disconnected,” exactly. That sounds bad. But there are times where I’m sure something is happening, even if other people tell me it’s not.\n\nLike one time my supervisor showed me there wasn’t anything on the camera, and I could see what he was saying… but I still felt like the building people had marked me somehow. Like the hallway lights, and little looks they gave each other, were warnings about me.\n\nI know that probably doesn’t sound right. I’m just trying to explain it honestly. Is that the kind of thing you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a problem with alcohol or drug use in the past?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so, Doctor. I mean, I’ve had drinks here and there like people do, but I wouldn’t say it was a problem.\n\nAnd drugs, no, not really. I don’t want to make it sound like I’m perfect or anything, but I can’t think of a time where alcohol or drugs were the reason things were going wrong. Is that answering it the right way?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like you needed to cut down on your drinking or drug use?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I mean… I’ve never felt like I had to cut down, not in that way.\n\nI don’t really use drugs, and drinking isn’t something I feel pulled to do. So I don’t think that’s been an issue for me. Is that okay to say?\n\nDoctor: Do you have difficulty completing tasks or projects due to your thoughts being disorganized?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that does happen. I don’t mean to be difficult or anything, but when I try to explain something, it can get all tangled up.\n\nLike I’ll start talking about the keys, and then somehow I’m on jail, and then the prayer rug, and then chess pieces… and in my head it feels connected, but people tell me they can’t follow what I’m saying. So I guess, yes, it makes it hard to finish what I’m trying to do or explain.\n\nI’m trying to answer it the right way.\n\nDoctor: Do you have times when your thoughts are organized and you can express yourself clearly?\n\nPatient: Sometimes, yeah… I think there are times I can say things clear enough. I don’t want to make it sound like I’m always mixed up.\n\nBut what brings me here is that my talking does get all tangled. I try to explain about the keys, and then it goes to the jail, then the prayer rug, then chess pieces… and people tell me they can’t follow me. In my head I’m trying to make sense, but it doesn’t always come out that way.\n\nIs that what you mean, Doctor?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your mixed-up thoughts and speech are causing you a lot of distress or problems in your daily life or relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it has caused problems, more than I like to admit.\n\nI try to keep it together, but I’ve missed shifts and lost some cleaning work because people said they couldn’t rely on me, or they couldn’t understand what I was trying to say. And I stopped going to mosque sometimes, and family gatherings too, because I feel embarrassed… like people are looking at me or getting frustrated.\n\nThere have been times it got bad enough that other people had to call for emergency help. I don’t like saying that, Doctor, but… yes, it has affected my life a lot. Is that the kind of thing you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any extremely stressful events or significant changes in your life around the time your speech started getting mixed up?\n\nPatient: I think… yeah, there was something around then. I don’t want to make it sound like an excuse, Doctor, but after that brief detention, the misunderstanding at the building… that’s when I started noticing my speech getting more mixed up.\n\nIt’s been going on about… two years and five months now, I think. Around there. I hope that answers what you’re asking.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any other symptoms like the voice you mentioned, such as seeing things that aren't there, besides the voice you hear?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I really see things, Doctor… not like that. At least, I don’t want to say something wrong.\n\nIt’s more the voice. Sometimes I hear a man’s voice coming from the vents, or from the mop closet, saying my name… or saying I ruined everything. And it sounds real, like someone is standing right there beside me. I know that probably sounds bad, but that’s how it feels when it happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you have times when the voice you hear and the mixed-up thoughts go away completely?\n\nPatient: Not completely, no… I mean, there are quieter times, and I try to act normal and get through the day.\n\nBut the voice, and those worries that I’m being watched or kind of… marked somehow, they’ve been around maybe a year and a half. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, Doctor, but they don’t really go away all the way. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you think the voice you hear is real or just a product of your mind?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that the right way, Doctor. Part of me knows maybe it could be my mind… I don’t want to sound like I’m arguing with you.\n\nBut when it happens, it feels real. Like I’ll hear a man’s voice coming from the vents or the mop closet, saying my name, or saying I ruined everything. In that moment it sounds as real as someone standing right beside me."
